Traditional defenses, which instruct agents to disregard deceptive elements, have not been effective. The authors introduce a novel approach termed "in-context defense." This defense mechanism utilizes in-context learning and chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, enhancing the agent's context with curated exemplars that illustrate both deceptive scenarios and appropriate defensive responses. This method enables agents to adopt a defensive reasoning stance prior to engaged action planning, significantly improving resilience against attacks. The experimental results show a remarkable reduction in success rates of context deception attacks: a 91.2% reduction for pop-up window attacks, an average of 74.6% for environment injection attacks, and irrefutable defense against distracting advertisements. The study emphasizes that effective defense necessitates prior defensive reasoning, and notably, fewer than three exemplars are sufficient for inducing the desired defensive behavior in agents. ### Critical Evaluation **Novelty:**  The paper presents a meaningful contribution by introducing in-context defense, a new strategy for mitigating context deception attacks in VLM-powered computer agents. This approach is both timely and necessary since human-computer interaction increasingly relies on such models. The integration of in-context learning with CoT reasoning marks a progression beyond existing defensive measures, which are largely ineffective. It distinguishes itself through a systematic study specifically addressing the gap in defenses against context deception attacks. **Significance:** The findings highlight crucial insights into the dynamics of agent behavior in adversarial contexts, pushing the boundaries of existing knowledge on effective defensive strategies in AI systems. By demonstrating a drastic reduction in attack success rates, the implication of enhancing agent security is significant for developing safer AI applications. **Strengths:** 1. **Empirical Validation:** The paper is grounded in rigorous experimentation, providing substantial quantitative backing for the proposed method. 2. **Innovative Approach:** The use of curated exemplars as a mechanism for teaching agents defensive reasoning is a novel methodology that could inspire further research in the field. **Weaknesses:** 1. **Limited Scope of Experiments:** Although the results are impressive, the paper could be criticized for lacking diverse scenarios and broader environmental complexities in testing, which may not fully represent real-world situations. 2. **Generality of Defense Mechanism:** The proposed approach may be context-specific; its effectiveness across various types of deceptive attacks or other AI architectures remains to be fully explored. In conclusion, the paper contributes substantially to the field by addressing a relevant security challenge and providing a novel solution grounded in empirical evidence. However, its practical applications may need further exploration to ensure robustness across diverse contexts. **Score: 8**  This score reflects a strong innovative contribution with solid empirical evidence but acknowledges the potential limitations in scope and generalizability that would benefit from more comprehensive exploration.
- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### NAMI: Efficient Image Generation via Progressive Rectified Flow Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09242v1)
- **Authors**: Yuhang Ma, Bo Cheng, Shanyuan Liu, Ao Ma, Xiaoyu Wu, Liebucha Wu, Dawei Leng, Yuhui Yin
- **Abstract**: Flow-based transformer models for image generation have achieved state-of-the-art performance with larger model parameters, but their inference deployment cost remains high. To enhance inference performance while maintaining generation quality, we propose progressive rectified flow transformers. We divide the rectified flow into different stages according to resolution, using fewer transformer layers at the low-resolution stages to generate image layouts and concept contours, and progressively adding more layers as the resolution increases. Experiments demonstrate that our approach achieves fast convergence and reduces inference time while ensuring generation quality. The main contributions of this paper are summarized as follows: (1) We introduce progressive rectified flow transformers that enable multi-resolution training, accelerating model convergence; (2) NAMI leverages piecewise flow and spatial cascading of Diffusion Transformer (DiT) to rapidly generate images, reducing inference time by 40% to generate a 1024 resolution image; (3) We propose NAMI-1K benchmark to evaluate human preference performance, aiming to mitigate distributional bias and prevent data leakage from open-source benchmarks. The results show that our model is competitive with state-of-the-art models.
- **Summary**: ### Summary of the Paper The paper titled "NAMI: Efficient Image Generation via Progressive Rectified Flow Transformers" addresses the high deployment costs associated with flow-based transformer models for image generation. The authors propose a novel approach called progressive rectified flow transformers, which organize the image generation process into multiple stages based on resolution. In the lower-resolution stages, they utilize fewer transformer layers to generate basic layouts and outlines, incrementally adding more layers as the resolution increases. This method not only accelerates model convergence but also considerably reduces inference time—illustrated by a 40% decrease for generating 1024x1024 resolution images. Additionally, the paper introduces the NAMI-1K benchmark for evaluating models with respect to human preference, focusing on mitigating biases commonly found in open-source datasets. The experiments conducted validate the effectiveness of their proposed approach, demonstrating competitive performance against state-of-the-art models while maintaining rapid inference capabilities. ### Critical Evaluation 1. **Novelty**: The concept of using multi-resolution training with progressive addition of transformer layers is a noteworthy innovation, particularly in the context of flow-based models where inference costs are a known bottleneck. This approach appears to differentiate NAMI from existing models that typically employ uniform architectures across resolutions. Furthermore, the introduction of the NAMI-1K benchmark is significant, as it addresses critical evaluation issues within the field. 2. **Significance**: The model promises practical improvements in inference speed while retaining quality, which is particularly beneficial for real-world applications where deployment efficiency is crucial. The reduction in inference time (40% for high-resolution images) is a compelling advantage that can help facilitate the adoption of complex models in resource-constrained environments.  3. **Clarity of Contribution**: While the contributions are clearly outlined and supported with experimental results, one concern lies in the generalizability of these findings. The reduction in inference time and enhanced generation quality should be evaluated across a wider array of datasets and image types to assess robustness. Moreover, additional comparative analysis with a broader set of existing models—particularly those that also focus on efficiency—would strengthen the claims made regarding the superiority of NAMI. 4. **Potential Influence**: The focus on predictive efficiency combined with an effort to benchmark human preference indicates a forward-thinking mindset that could influence future research directions in the field of generative models. Nevertheless, the impact will depend heavily on how well the community receives the proposed NAMI-1K benchmark and its implementation across varied applications. ### Conclusion Overall, the paper presents a compelling case for an innovative method that retains the performance benefits of established models while improving efficiency for practical applications. However, further validation is required to ensure its robustness and superiority relative to existing solutions. Given these considerations, I would assign the paper a score of 8. **Score: 8**
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### DeepInnovation AI: A Global Dataset Mapping the AI innovation and technology Transfer from Academic Research to Industrial Patents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09257v1)
- **Authors**: Haixing Gong, Hui Zou, Xingzhou Liang, Shiyuan Meng, Pinlong Cai, Xingcheng Xu, Jingjing Qu
- **Abstract**: In the rapidly evolving field of artificial intelligence (AI), mapping innovation patterns and understanding effective technology transfer from academic research to practical applications are essential for economic growth. This paper introduces DeepInnovationAI, the first comprehensive global dataset designed to bridge the gap between academic papers and industrial patents. However, existing data infrastructures face three major limitations: fragmentation, incomplete coverage, and insufficient evaluative capacity. Here, we present DeepInnovationAI, a comprehensive global dataset documenting AI innovation trajectories. The dataset comprises three structured files: DeepPatentAI.csv: Contains 2,356,204 patent records with 8 field-specific attributes. DeepDiveAI.csv: Encompasses 3,511,929 academic publications with 13 metadata fields. These two datasets employ large language models, multilingual text analysis and dual-layer BERT classifiers to accurately identify AI-related content and utilizing hypergraph analysis methods to create robust innovation metrics. In addition, DeepCosineAI.csv: By applying semantic vector proximity analysis, this file presents approximately one hundred million calculated paper-patent similarity pairs to enhance understanding of how theoretical advancements translate into commercial technologies. This enables researchers, policymakers, and industry leaders to anticipate trends and identify emerging areas for collaboration. With its extensive temporal and geographical scope, DeepInnovationAI supports detailed analysis of technological development patterns and international competition dynamics, providing a robust foundation for modeling AI innovation dynamics and technology transfer processes.
- **Summary**: ### Summary of the Paper The paper "DeepInnovation AI: A Global Dataset Mapping the AI Innovation and Technology Transfer from Academic Research to Industrial Patents" introduces DeepInnovationAI, a pioneering comprehensive global dataset aimed at enhancing the understanding of AI innovation patterns and facilitating technology transfer between academic research and industrial applications. The authors identify three key limitations of current data infrastructures in this domain: fragmentation, incomplete coverage, and inadequate evaluative capacity.  DeepInnovationAI consists of three structured files: 1. **DeepPatentAI.csv** - Contains over 2.3 million patent records with specific fields relevant to AI. 2. **DeepDiveAI.csv** - Comprises more than 3.5 million academic publications, enriched with multiple metadata attributes. 3. **DeepCosineAI.csv** - Features approximately 100 million calculated similarity pairs between academic papers and patents, leveraging semantic vector proximity analysis. The methodology employs advanced language models and hypergraph analysis to uncover robust innovation metrics, promoting insights for researchers, policymakers, and industry stakeholders. The broad coverage of both temporal and geographic data enables detailed analyses of technology trends and competitive dynamics in AI. --- ### Critical Evaluation #### Novelty and Significance **Strengths:** 1. **Data Integration:** The paper provides a unique integration of academic research and industrial patents, addressing a crucial gap in understanding how theoretical advancements in AI transition to tangible technologies. 2. **Comprehensive Dataset:** The scale and structure of the dataset (over 2.3 million patents and over 3.5 million publications) are impressive and offer substantial opportunities for various stakeholders to analyze AI innovation trajectories. 3. **Advanced Methodologies:** The employment of large language models, BERT classification, and hypergraph analysis for establishing innovation metrics represents a significant advancement in how researchers can quantify and assess AI innovations. **Weaknesses:** 1. **Evaluative Capacity Limitation:** While the dataset enhances understanding, the paper does not sufficiently detail how its evaluative capabilities will concretely impact decision-making in industry or policy. 2. **Potential Biases in Data Collection:** The paper does not discuss the specific methodologies used to compile and validate the dataset, which raises concerns about potential biases and data integrity. 3. **Dependence on Technological Advancements:** The insights generated from the dataset may be overly reliant on the capabilities of current AI methodologies, potentially limiting its applicability in future contexts as technology evolves. #### Potential Influence The dataset has the potential to influence various stakeholders significantly, enabling better predictions about trends in AI technology and fostering collaboration between sectors. However, the impact hinges on how effectively researchers and practitioners utilize this resource and address the limitations related to data collection biases and evaluative capacity. **Score Justification:** Taking into account the innovative aspects, breadth of data, and methodologies introduced, along with the existing limitations regarding evaluative capacity and potential biases, I would assign the paper a moderate to high score. **Score: 7**  This score reflects a significant contribution to the field of AI research and innovation mapping while acknowledging areas where further refinement and validation are necessary for the dataset to achieve its full potential.
- **Classification**: cs.DB
- **Score**: 7/10

### COLA: A Scalable Multi-Agent Framework For Windows UI Task Automation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09263v1)
- **Authors**: Di Zhao, Longhui Ma, Siwei Wang, Miao Wang, Zhao Lv
- **Abstract**: With the rapid advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs), an increasing number of studies have leveraged LLMs as the cognitive core of agents to address complex task decision-making challenges. Specially, recent research has demonstrated the potential of LLM-based agents on automating Windows GUI operations. However, existing methodologies exhibit two critical challenges: (1) static agent architectures fail to dynamically adapt to the heterogeneous requirements of OS-level tasks, leading to inadequate scenario generalization;(2) the agent workflows lack fault tolerance mechanism, necessitating complete process re-execution for UI agent decision error. To address these limitations, we introduce \textit{COLA}, a collaborative multi-agent framework for automating Windows UI operations. In this framework, a scenario-aware agent Task Scheduler decomposes task requirements into atomic capability units, dynamically selects the optimal agent from a decision agent pool, effectively responds to the capability requirements of diverse scenarios. The decision agent pool supports plug-and-play expansion for enhanced flexibility. In addition, we design a memory unit equipped to all agents for their self-evolution. Furthermore, we develop an interactive backtracking mechanism that enables human to intervene to trigger state rollbacks for non-destructive process repair. Our experimental results on the GAIA benchmark demonstrates that the \textit{COLA} framework achieves state-of-the-art performance with an average score of 31.89\%, significantly outperforming baseline approaches without web API integration. Ablation studies further validate the individual contributions of our dynamic scheduling. The code is available at https://github.com/Alokia/COLA-demo.
- **Summary**: ### Summary The paper presents COLA, a scalable multi-agent framework designed for automating Windows UI tasks, leveraging advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) for improved task decision-making. The authors identify two major challenges in existing methodologies: static agent architectures that do not adapt to varying OS-level task requirements and a lack of fault tolerance in agent workflows, necessitating complete re-execution after errors. To overcome these issues, COLA features a scenario-aware Task Scheduler that breaks down tasks into manageable units and selects the optimal agent from a flexible pool, allowing for better adaptability. The framework also includes a memory unit for self-evolution of agents and an interactive backtracking mechanism for human intervention, providing non-destructive error recovery. Experimental results show that COLA achieves a significant performance improvement over previous approaches, particularly in the GAIA benchmark, and the authors provide a detailed analysis of their framework's components and contributions. ### Critical Evaluation **Novelty and Significance**:  The paper introduces important contributions to the field of task automation, particularly by addressing key limitations in existing multi-agent frameworks. The implementation of a scenario-aware Task Scheduler allows agents to dynamically respond to varying task requirements—an innovation that enhances adaptability and effectiveness in real-world applications. Furthermore, the addition of memory units and interactive backtracking mechanisms shows a promising approach to improving error handling and fostering agent evolution, which could lead to more resilient systems in the face of complex tasks. **Strengths**: 1. **Innovation**: The dynamic selection of agents in response to task requirements is a novel approach compared to traditional static frameworks. 2. **Scalability**: The framework allows for a plug-and-play mechanism to expand the decision agent pool, making COLA adaptable to future requirements. 3. **Error Recovery**: The incorporation of interactive backtracking enhances user control during error states, which is a significant improvement over previous methodologies that required restarting processes. **Weaknesses**: 1. **Complexity**: While the system's adaptability is a strength, it could also introduce complexity in implementation and training of agents, making it less accessible for users not familiar with advanced AI systems. 2. **Generalizability of Results**: The benchmark used (GAIA) may not cover the breadth of real-world use cases, potentially limiting the generalizability of the results. Further testing in diverse environments would strengthen credibility. 3. **Limited Context on Performance Metrics**: The reported average score of 31.89% lacks contextual comparison regarding what percentage constitutes state-of-the-art, reducing clarity on performance significance. **Overall Influence on the Field**: Considering the increasing reliance on automation in various industries, the proposed framework has the potential to influence future developments in agent-based system designs and GUI automation. The frameworks could inspire further research focusing on both dynamic adaptability and robust error recovery methods in automation. **Final Evaluation Score**:  Given the strengths of this innovative approach, the importance of addressing existing limitations in automation frameworks, and the experimental validation presented, I assign a score of **8**. This score reflects a solid contribution to the field, with room for improvement in terms of clarity of impact metrics and further expansion of the benchmark applicability.  **Score: 8**
- **Classification**: cs.MA
- **Score**: 8/10

### Fine-Tuning Large Language Models for Educational Support: Leveraging Gagne's Nine Events of Instruction for Lesson Planning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09276v1)
- **Authors**: Linzhao Jia, Changyong Qi, Yuang Wei, Han Sun, Xiaozhe Yang
- **Abstract**: Effective lesson planning is crucial in education process, serving as the cornerstone for high-quality teaching and the cultivation of a conducive learning atmosphere. This study investigates how large language models (LLMs) can enhance teacher preparation by incorporating them with Gagne's Nine Events of Instruction, especially in the field of mathematics education in compulsory education. It investigates two distinct methodologies: the development of Chain of Thought (CoT) prompts to direct LLMs in generating content that aligns with instructional events, and the application of fine-tuning approaches like Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) to enhance model performance. This research starts with creating a comprehensive dataset based on math curriculum standards and Gagne's instructional events. The first method involves crafting CoT-optimized prompts to generate detailed, logically coherent responses from LLMs, improving their ability to create educationally relevant content. The second method uses specialized datasets to fine-tune open-source models, enhancing their educational content generation and analysis capabilities. This study contributes to the evolving dialogue on the integration of AI in education, illustrating innovative strategies for leveraging LLMs to bolster teaching and learning processes.
- **Summary**: **Summary**:  The paper investigates the application of large language models (LLMs) to enhance lesson planning through the framework of Gagne's Nine Events of Instruction, specifically within mathematics education for compulsory schooling. It explores two main methodologies: (1) crafting Chain of Thought (CoT) prompts aimed at generating coherent educational content that aligns with instructional events, and (2) fine-tuning techniques like Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) to improve LLM performance in generating educational materials. A dataset has been developed based on math curriculum standards and Gagne's framework to support the research. This work contributes to the conversation about integrating AI in education by showcasing innovative methods for utilizing LLMs to improve teaching and learning processes. --- **Evaluation**:  This paper presents a commendable approach to integrating LLMs into educational practices. Its focus on lesson planning—a critical component of effective teaching—highlights a significant area of need within education, particularly amidst the growing reliance on AI tools. **Strengths**: 1. **Relevance**: The use of LLMs in educational contexts is timely and addresses current trends in AI application. 2. **Framework Utilization**: Leveraging Gagne's Nine Events of Instruction provides a well-established pedagogical framework that enhances the paper’s theoretical grounding. 3. **Methodological Innovation**: The combination of CoT prompting and fine-tuning techniques represents a novel approach to improving LLM outputs for educational purposes, facilitating more tailored and effective lesson planning. **Weaknesses**: 1. **Generalizability**: The focus on mathematics education may restrict broader applicability. It would be beneficial to explore how these methods can be applied across various subject areas. 2. **Empirical Validation**: The paper could be strengthened with empirical data showcasing the effectiveness of the proposed methodologies in real educational settings, including teacher and student feedback. 3. **Long-Term Impact**: While the study outlines methods for immediate enhancement of lesson planning, it does not sufficiently address the long-term sustainability and integration of LLMs within traditional teaching environments. **Conclusion**:  Overall, the research makes a valuable contribution to the field of educational technology by proposing practical methods for enhancing lesson planning through LLMs. However, it could benefit from broader application and more empirical evidence to support its claims. **Score: 7**  This score reflects the paper's significant contributions to the field, alongside its limitations in empirical evidence and broader applicability, ultimately positioning it as a promising but still developing investigation in the educational landscape.
- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### UniCombine: Unified Multi-Conditional Combination with Diffusion Transformer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09277v1)
- **Authors**: Haoxuan Wang, Jinlong Peng, Qingdong He, Hao Yang, Ying Jin, Jiafu Wu, Xiaobin Hu, Yanjie Pan, Zhenye Gan, Mingmin Chi, Bo Peng, Yabiao Wang
- **Abstract**: With the rapid development of diffusion models in image generation, the demand for more powerful and flexible controllable frameworks is increasing. Although existing methods can guide generation beyond text prompts, the challenge of effectively combining multiple conditional inputs while maintaining consistency with all of them remains unsolved. To address this, we introduce UniCombine, a DiT-based multi-conditional controllable generative framework capable of handling any combination of conditions, including but not limited to text prompts, spatial maps, and subject images. Specifically, we introduce a novel Conditional MMDiT Attention mechanism and incorporate a trainable LoRA module to build both the training-free and training-based versions. Additionally, we propose a new pipeline to construct SubjectSpatial200K, the first dataset designed for multi-conditional generative tasks covering both the subject-driven and spatially-aligned conditions. Extensive experimental results on multi-conditional generation demonstrate the outstanding universality and powerful capability of our approach with state-of-the-art performance.
- **Summary**: ### Summary: The paper titled "UniCombine: Unified Multi-Conditional Combination with Diffusion Transformer" presents a novel generative framework, named UniCombine, designed to enhance the control and flexibility of image generation using diffusion models. The authors acknowledge the existing limitations in effectively merging multiple conditional inputs—such as text prompts, spatial maps, and subject images—while maintaining coherence in the generated outputs.  UniCombine addresses this issue through the introduction of a new Conditional MMDiT (Multi-Modal Diffusion Transformer) Attention mechanism and a trainable LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) module, which allows for both training-free and training-based implementations. The paper also describes the creation of SubjectSpatial200K, a dataset specifically built for multi-conditional generative tasks, encompassing both subject-driven and spatially-aligned conditions. The experimental results demonstrate that UniCombine achieves state-of-the-art performance across various multi-conditional generation scenarios, highlighting its versatility and robustness. ### Evaluation: **Strengths:** 1. **Novelty:** UniCombine introduces a fresh conceptual approach to combining multi-conditional inputs in diffusion models, addressing a significant gap in the existing literature where the coherence of outputs based on multiple conditions is often a challenge. The Conditional MMDiT Attention mechanism is a promising advancement in attention mechanisms tailored for generative tasks. 2. **Dataset Contribution:** The creation of the SubjectSpatial200K dataset is an essential contribution to the field, as it provides a resource for future multi-conditional generative studies, which can facilitate further advancements in this area. 3. **Empirical Validation:** The extensive experiments demonstrate the framework's effectiveness across diverse conditions, suggesting that it has practical relevance and could be utilized for various applications in computer vision. 4. **Implementation Flexibility:** Offering both training-free and training-based versions enhances usability and allows researchers with varying resources to engage with the framework. **Weaknesses:** 1. **Depth of Technical Details:** While the paper presents a novel approach, the technical depth provided in the methodological explanation (particularly around the implementation of the MMDiT mechanism) could have been elaborated further. This would allow other researchers to better understand and reproduce the results. 2. **Comparative Analysis:** Although the paper shows strong performance, the comparison with a wider range of existing state-of-the-art models could strengthen the claims of superiority. A more detailed ablation study exploring various combinations of conditions might also provide deeper insights into the framework's operation. 3. **Generalizability Beyond Images:** The focus on image generation implies a limited scope. The potential for applying the proposed methods to different modalities (e.g., text or audio) could be discussed, expanding the relevance of the work beyond image-focused tasks. Overall, the paper presents a compelling advancement in the field of controllable generation with diffusion models. It introduces innovative mechanisms and datasets that will likely inspire further research. However, it also leaves certain technical details vague, limiting its immediate applicability. **Score: 8**  This score reflects a strong but not groundbreaking contribution, recognizing the innovation and potential impact while also acknowledging the need for more rigorous comparative analysis and technical depth to elevate the findings.
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Crowdsourced Homophily Ties Based Graph Annotation Via Large Language Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09281v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Bu, Yulin Zhu, Kai Zhou
- **Abstract**: Accurate graph annotation typically requires substantial labeled data, which is often challenging and resource-intensive to obtain. In this paper, we present Crowdsourced Homophily Ties Based Graph Annotation via Large Language Model (CSA-LLM), a novel approach that combines the strengths of crowdsourced annotations with the capabilities of large language models (LLMs) to enhance the graph annotation process. CSA-LLM harnesses the structural context of graph data by integrating information from 1-hop and 2-hop neighbors. By emphasizing homophily ties - key connections that signify similarity within the graph - CSA-LLM significantly improves the accuracy of annotations. Experimental results demonstrate that this method enhances the performance of Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) by delivering more precise and reliable annotations.
- **Summary**: **Summary:** The paper titled "Crowdsourced Homophily Ties Based Graph Annotation Via Large Language Model (CSA-LLM)" introduces a new methodology for graph annotation that synergizes crowdsourced inputs with the analytical power of large language models (LLMs). This approach addresses the common challenge of obtaining sufficient labeled data for accurate graph annotations, which is often labor-intensive and costly. CSA-LLM innovatively leverages homophily ties—connections between similar nodes—by incorporating contextual information from both 1-hop and 2-hop neighbors in the graph structure. The authors demonstrate through experiments that this strategy significantly enhances the quality of annotations, leading to improved performance of Graph Neural Networks (GNNs). **Critical Evaluation:** The CSA-LLM paper presents a noteworthy contribution in the intersection of crowdsourcing, graph theory, and machine learning. Its novelty primarily lies in the integration of LLMs with graph annotation, which is a relatively underexplored domain, particularly in the context of leveraging crowd inputs. This blending of traditional graph concepts with modern LLM techniques opens avenues for more effective data labeling and enhances the use of GNNs, which remain a significant area of research in machine learning. **Strengths:** 1. **Innovative Approach:** Combining homophily ties with LLMs is a fresh idea that may lead to better understanding and utilization of graph structures.     2. **Empirical Validation:** The experiments outlined show tangible improvements in annotation accuracy and GNN performance, providing solid evidence of the method's effectiveness. 3. **Resource Efficiency:** By leveraging crowdsourcing, the paper may inspire future works that seek to minimize dependency on extensive manual labeling. **Weaknesses:** 1. **Scalability Concerns:** While the approach shows promising results, it does not address the scalability of crowdsourced data or how LLMs can manage larger, more diverse datasets effectively. 2. **Specificity of Homophily Ties:** The emphasis on homophily is beneficial, but the paper could benefit from exploring what happens in non-homophilic contexts or diverse graphs. 3. **Limited Scope:** The methodology’s applicability in varied domains beyond the tested settings is uncertain, and more generalizability could enhance its utility. Overall, the paper represents a meaningful advance in the graph annotation arena, with a careful design that is grounded in successful empirical testing. However, the issues of scalability and applicability raise questions about its broader impact. **Final Score: 7** This score reflects a solid contribution with innovative elements, yet it acknowledges limitations that temper its overall impact within the research community. The CSA-LLM has the potential to inspire future research but requires further testing and development to establish its robustness and general applicability across diverse graph-based applications.
- **Classification**: cs.SI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unmask It! AI-Generated Product Review Detection in Dravidian Languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09289v1)
- **Authors**: Somsubhra De, Advait Vats
- **Abstract**: The rise of Generative AI has led to a surge in AI-generated reviews, often posing a serious threat to the credibility of online platforms. Reviews serve as the primary source of information about products and services. Authentic reviews play a vital role in consumer decision-making. The presence of fabricated content misleads consumers, undermines trust and facilitates potential fraud in digital marketplaces. This study focuses on detecting AI-generated product reviews in Tamil and Malayalam, two low-resource languages where research in this domain is relatively under-explored. We worked on a range of approaches - from traditional machine learning methods to advanced transformer-based models such as Indic-BERT, IndicSBERT, MuRIL, XLM-RoBERTa and MalayalamBERT. Our findings highlight the effectiveness of leveraging the state-of-the-art transformers in accurately identifying AI-generated content, demonstrating the potential in enhancing the detection of fake reviews in low-resource language settings.
- **Summary**: **Summary:** The paper titled "Unmask It! AI-Generated Product Review Detection in Dravidian Languages" addresses the escalating issue of AI-generated reviews that threaten the credibility of online review systems. It emphasizes the importance of authentic reviews for consumer decision-making and examines the challenges posed by fake content in Tamil and Malayalam, two under-researched Dravidian languages. The authors explore various methodologies, including traditional machine learning and state-of-the-art transformer models like Indic-BERT and XLM-RoBERTa, to enhance the detection of AI-generated content. Their results suggest that employing these advanced models significantly improves the identification of fake reviews, showcasing their applicability in low-resource language contexts. **Critical Evaluation:** The paper's novelty lies in its focus on Dravidian languages, which are often overlooked in the broader discourse on AI-generated content detection. Research in this area has predominantly concentrated on high-resource languages like English, making the exploration of Tamil and Malayalam particularly relevant and timely. The use of cutting-edge transformer models to tackle this problem is commendable, as it incorporates advanced techniques into a relatively unexplored field. Strengths of the paper include its relevance to current digital marketplace challenges, the application of a range of modern methodologies, and the contribution to enhancing trust in online reviews specific to low-resource settings. The findings showcase not just the utility of these models but also their robustness in handling complex language structures and nuances inherent to Tamil and Malayalam. However, some weaknesses limit the overall impact of the study. Firstly, the paper could benefit from a more detailed explanation of the datasets utilized, including how the authenticity of reviews was determined. Secondly, the evaluation metrics for measuring the effectiveness of the AI-generated review detection methods are not discussed in depth, potentially leaving gaps in understanding the full implications of the results. The study also does not engage with the societal or ethical implications of deploying such technologies, which is crucial in discussions about AI in consumer contexts. Overall, while the paper makes a solid contribution to the field, its impact could be further amplified by addressing these issues. The balance of strengths and weaknesses leads to an overall score that reflects its significance but also recognizes the need for further refinement. **Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompt Inference Attack on Distributed Large Language Model Inference Frameworks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09291v1)
- **Authors**: Xinjian Luo, Ting Yu, Xiaokui Xiao
- **Abstract**: The inference process of modern large language models (LLMs) demands prohibitive computational resources, rendering them infeasible for deployment on consumer-grade devices. To address this limitation, recent studies propose distributed LLM inference frameworks, which employ split learning principles to enable collaborative LLM inference on resource-constrained hardware. However, distributing LLM layers across participants requires the transmission of intermediate outputs, which may introduce privacy risks to the original input prompts - a critical issue that has yet to be thoroughly explored in the literature. In this paper, we rigorously examine the privacy vulnerabilities of distributed LLM inference frameworks by designing and evaluating three prompt inference attacks aimed at reconstructing input prompts from intermediate LLM outputs. These attacks are developed under various query and data constraints to reflect diverse real-world LLM service scenarios. Specifically, the first attack assumes an unlimited query budget and access to an auxiliary dataset sharing the same distribution as the target prompts. The second attack also leverages unlimited queries but uses an auxiliary dataset with a distribution differing from the target prompts. The third attack operates under the most restrictive scenario, with limited query budgets and no auxiliary dataset available. We evaluate these attacks on a range of LLMs, including state-of-the-art models such as Llama-3.2 and Phi-3.5, as well as widely-used models like GPT-2 and BERT for comparative analysis. Our experiments show that the first two attacks achieve reconstruction accuracies exceeding 90%, while the third achieves accuracies typically above 50%, even under stringent constraints. These findings highlight privacy risks in distributed LLM inference frameworks, issuing a strong alert on their deployment in real-world applications.
- **Summary**: ### Summary of the Paper The paper titled "Prompt Inference Attack on Distributed Large Language Model Inference Frameworks" investigates privacy vulnerabilities associated with distributed inference frameworks for large language models (LLMs). These frameworks, designed to facilitate collaborative model inference on limited-resource hardware, require the transmission of intermediate outputs, which may inadvertently compromise the privacy of input prompts. The authors propose and assess three distinct prompt inference attacks:  1. The **first attack** assumes unlimited queries and access to an auxiliary dataset that shares the same distribution as the target prompts. 2. The **second attack** also employs unlimited queries but utilizes an auxiliary dataset with a differing distribution. 3. The **third attack** is conducted under constrained conditions, with limits on queries and no access to auxiliary datasets. Through experiments conducted on various LLMs, including state-of-the-art models like Llama-3.2 and Phi-3.5 alongside widely-used models such as GPT-2 and BERT, the authors report that the first two attacks achieve reconstruction accuracies exceeding 90%, while the third attack maintains accuracies above 50%. The findings underline significant privacy risks encountered in distributed LLM inference frameworks, raising concerns regarding their practical implementation. ### Critical Evaluation **Novelty:** The paper addresses a critical yet underserved area in the study of distributed LLM inference frameworks—privacy vulnerabilities. While prior works have explored aspects of LLM training and inference, few have delved deep into the privacy implications of disseminating intermediate outputs, making the focus of this research relatively innovative. **Significance:** The implications of the research are substantial, particularly as distributed computing systems for LLMs gain traction. Discovering that prompt inputs can be reconstructed with high accuracy under various conditions provides a necessary warning to researchers and practitioners in the field regarding the potential privacy risks involved in real-world applications of distributed LLM systems. **Strengths:** 1. **Comprehensive Approach:** The study employs a systematic attack strategy over varying conditions reflecting real-world applications, demonstrating thorough methodology. 2. **Empirical Evidence:** The high reconstruction accuracy rates reported add credibility to the paper, indicating significant vulnerabilities. 3. **Timely Research:** With the rise of collaborative deep learning, this research is timely and relevant. **Weaknesses:** 1. **Generalizability:** While the research focuses on a range of LLMs, the specific conditions and settings in which attacks yielded high success may limit generalizability beyond the tested models. 2. **Implications and Mitigation Strategies:** The paper presents significant findings regarding privacy vulnerabilities but falls short of providing solutions or mitigation strategies, which could have strengthened its impact and utility. 3. **Lack of Depth in Ethical Considerations:** While it raises awareness of privacy issues, the paper does not explore the ethical ramifications of such vulnerabilities in depth. In conclusion, the paper makes a meaningful and significant contribution to understanding privacy risks in distributed LLM inference frameworks. Despite some limitations, particularly in generalizability and the lack of mitigation discussion, the focus on real-world implications sets a strong foundation for future research in safeguarding privacy while utilizing distributed LLMs. **Score: 8**
- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Priority-Aware Preemptive Scheduling for Mixed-Priority Workloads in MoE Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09304v1)
- **Authors**: Mohammad Siavashi, Faezeh Keshmiri Dindarloo, Dejan Kostic, Marco Chiesa
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models have revolutionized natural language processing, yet serving them efficiently in data centers remains challenging due to mixed workloads comprising latency-sensitive (LS) and best-effort (BE) jobs. Existing inference systems employ iteration-level first-come-first-served scheduling, causing head-of-line blocking when BE jobs delay LS jobs. We introduce QLLM, a novel inference system designed for Mixture of Experts (MoE) models, featuring a fine-grained, priority-aware preemptive scheduler. QLLM enables expert-level preemption, deferring BE job execution while minimizing LS time-to-first-token (TTFT). Our approach removes iteration-level scheduling constraints, enabling the scheduler to preempt jobs at any layer based on priority. Evaluations on an Nvidia A100 GPU show that QLLM significantly improves performance. It reduces LS TTFT by an average of $65.5\times$ and meets the SLO at up to $7$ requests/sec, whereas the baseline fails to do so under the tested workload. Additionally, it cuts LS turnaround time by up to $12.8\times$ without impacting throughput. QLLM is modular, extensible, and seamlessly integrates with Hugging Face MoE models.
- **Summary**: ### Summary of the Paper The paper introduces QLLM, a novel inference system tailored for Mixture of Experts (MoE) models, addressing the challenges posed by mixed workloads of latency-sensitive (LS) and best-effort (BE) jobs in data centers. Traditional inference systems rely on iteration-level first-come-first-served scheduling, which can lead to head-of-line blocking, particularly affecting LS job execution. QLLM employs a fine-grained, priority-aware preemptive scheduler that allows for expert-level preemption, thus enabling BE jobs to be deferred in favor of minimizing the time-to-first-token (TTFT) for LS jobs. By removing iteration-level scheduling constraints, QLLM can preempt jobs at any layer based on their priority. Evaluation results show a dramatic improvement in LS TTFT, averaging a reduction of 65.5 times, and a substantial improvement in LS turnaround time, without impacting throughput. QLLM integrates well with existing Hugging Face MoE models and demonstrates significant potential for enhancing performance in data centers serving large language models. ### Critical Evaluation **Strengths:** 1. **Addressing a Major Challenge:** The paper tackles a significant issue in the deployment of large language models—efficient scheduling in the face of mixed workloads. This is particularly relevant given the rising demand for low-latency responses in services powered by these models.     2. **Novel Scheduler Design:** The proposed fine-grained, priority-aware preemptive scheduler represents a clear innovation over traditional iteration-level scheduling approaches, providing a new mechanism for prioritizing latency-sensitive tasks and mitigating head-of-line blocking. 3. **Impressive Performance Metrics:** The reported improvements regarding the reduction in LS TTFT and turnaround time are substantial and suggest that the system could lead to very positive operational impacts in real-world deployment. 4. **Modularity and Integration:** The modularity of QLLM and its ability to integrate seamlessly with Hugging Face MoE models enhance its applicability and usefulness in existing frameworks. **Weaknesses:** 1. **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation is primarily conducted on an Nvidia A100 GPU, potentially limiting generalizability to other hardware or architectures. The performance gains may vary significantly with different configurations, which may not be addressed in the study. 2. **Lack of Comprehensive Comparisons:** While a baseline is mentioned, the paper could benefit from a more diverse comparison against various scheduling algorithms beyond basic iteration-level approaches. Including results from state-of-the-art systems would provide a clearer picture of QLLM's standing. 3. **Real-world Deployment Considerations:** While QLLM shows theoretical and empirical improvements, the paper does not sufficiently discuss the practical aspects of deploying the proposed scheduler in diverse operational environments, which might impact its broader adoption. 4. **Potential for Overhead:** The fine-grained preemption mechanism, while innovative, could introduce overhead that must be managed to ensure the benefits are realized in dynamic environments. ### Conclusion Overall, QLLM presents a promising advancement in the field of scheduling for mixed-priority workloads, specifically within natural language processing applications. Its innovative approach to prioritization and the strong performance metrics indicate that it could influence how large models are served in data centers. However, the evaluation's limitations and potential concerns regarding generalizability and real-world applications detract from the paper's overall impact. **Score: 7/10** - This score reflects a solid contribution to the field, particularly concerning scheduling and efficiency for MoE models, but the identified weaknesses warrant a cautious yet optimistic view regarding its broader significance and application.
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Revealing Unintentional Information Leakage in Low-Dimensional Facial Portrait Representations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09306v1)
- **Authors**: Kathleen Anderson, Thomas Martinetz
- **Abstract**: We evaluate the information that can unintentionally leak into the low dimensional output of a neural network, by reconstructing an input image from a 40- or 32-element feature vector that intends to only describe abstract attributes of a facial portrait. The reconstruction uses blackbox-access to the image encoder which generates the feature vector. Other than previous work, we leverage recent knowledge about image generation and facial similarity, implementing a method that outperforms the current state-of-the-art. Our strategy uses a pretrained StyleGAN and a new loss function that compares the perceptual similarity of portraits by mapping them into the latent space of a FaceNet embedding. Additionally, we present a new technique that fuses the output of an ensemble, to deliberately generate specific aspects of the recreated image.
- **Summary**: **Summary of the Paper:** The paper addresses the issue of unintentional information leakage in low-dimensional facial representations generated by neural networks. The authors focus on reconstructing original images from 40- or 32-element feature vectors that are intended to abstractly represent facial attributes. By utilizing blackbox access to the image encoder, they demonstrate their method's capability to outperform state-of-the-art reconstruction techniques. Key advancements include a novel loss function that assesses perceptual similarity through a FaceNet embedding in conjunction with a StyleGAN for image generation. Moreover, the authors introduce an ensemble output fusion technique aimed at enhancing the specificity of generated image attributes. **Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:** **Strengths:** 1. **Innovative Approach:** The paper's application of recent developments in image generation (e.g., StyleGAN) and loss function design showcases a significant step forward in the field of image reconstruction from low-dimensional embeddings.  2. **Attention to Perceptual Similarity:** The method's emphasis on perceptual similarity instead of mere pixel-wise accuracy indicates a more human-centric approach to image evaluation, which can be more relevant in applications such as photos and security systems. 3. **Technical Rigor:** The analysis of ensemble techniques to enhance image recreation is a sophisticated addition that might resonate with practitioners in the field looking for more precise control over generative outputs. **Weaknesses:** 1. **Limited Scope:** While addressing information leakage is crucial, the paper may benefit from a broader analysis of how this unintentional leakage impacts downstream applications such as facial recognition or privacy issues. A detailed discussion on real-world implications could enhance its significance. 2. **Evaluation Metrics:** Although the authors mention outperforming state-of-the-art methods, a clearer and more comprehensive comparison with baseline models could strengthen their claims about superiority. Detailed quantitative results alongside qualitative assessments would lend more weight to their conclusions. 3. **Generalizability:** The paper focuses on facial representations. It remains unclear how applicable the method is to various other domains or types of images, which may limit its impact. **Potential Influence:** The paper contributes positively to ongoing discourse around data privacy in neural networks and the integrity of machine learning models. By showcasing how feature encoders may leak sensitive information, it raises awareness important for developing more secure models. However, its influence could be further amplified with implementation recommendations and broader applicability discussions. **Score: 7** The score reflects solid contributions in methodology and a thoughtful approach to perceptual similarity. However, the narrow application scope, lack of extensive evaluation metrics, and the absence of discussions on broader implications mitigate the overall novelty and significance. Thus, while the paper holds merit, its full impact on the field may be somewhat constrained unless supplemented by additional insights and practical recommendations.
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Adaptive political surveys and GPT-4: Tackling the cold start problem with simulated user interactions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09311v1)
- **Authors**: Fynn Bachmann, Daan van der Weijden, Lucien Heitz, Cristina Sarasua, Abraham Bernstein
- **Abstract**: Adaptive questionnaires dynamically select the next question for a survey participant based on their previous answers. Due to digitalisation, they have become a viable alternative to traditional surveys in application areas such as political science. One limitation, however, is their dependency on data to train the model for question selection. Often, such training data (i.e., user interactions) are unavailable a priori. To address this problem, we (i) test whether Large Language Models (LLM) can accurately generate such interaction data and (ii) explore if these synthetic data can be used to pre-train the statistical model of an adaptive political survey. To evaluate this approach, we utilise existing data from the Swiss Voting Advice Application (VAA) Smartvote in two ways: First, we compare the distribution of LLM-generated synthetic data to the real distribution to assess its similarity. Second, we compare the performance of an adaptive questionnaire that is randomly initialised with one pre-trained on synthetic data to assess their suitability for training. We benchmark these results against an "oracle" questionnaire with perfect prior knowledge. We find that an off-the-shelf LLM (GPT-4) accurately generates answers to the Smartvote questionnaire from the perspective of different Swiss parties. Furthermore, we demonstrate that initialising the statistical model with synthetic data can (i) significantly reduce the error in predicting user responses and (ii) increase the candidate recommendation accuracy of the VAA. Our work emphasises the considerable potential of LLMs to create training data to improve the data collection process in adaptive questionnaires in LLM-affine areas such as political surveys.
- **Summary**: **Summary of the Paper:** The paper investigates the use of adaptive surveys, particularly in political science, where the next question in a survey is dynamically selected based on prior responses. One challenge such surveys face is the need for training data to effectively model question selection, which is often not available beforehand. To address this "cold start" problem, the authors examine whether Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically GPT-4, can generate synthetic user interaction data that could be used to pre-train adaptive political survey models. They analyze existing data from the Swiss Voting Advice Application (VAA) Smartvote to compare the characteristics of LLM-generated data with actual data. Their findings show that GPT-4 can simulate relevant responses, significantly enhancing model performance in terms of predicting user responses and improving candidate recommendation accuracy. The study underscores the potential of LLMs in enriching data collection for adaptive questionnaires in fields reliant on nuanced participant insights, such as political surveys. **Evaluation and Score:** The novelty of the paper lies in its exploration of using synthetic data generated by an LLM to address a specific gap in adaptive survey methodologies. This approach is innovative as it leverages advancements in AI to overcome traditional limitations in data availability, specifically in political science applications. The authors' use of an established framework (the Smartvote data) to validate their results adds credibility and demonstrates practical implications for real-world applications in political surveys. Strengths: 1. **Innovative Approach**: The application of LLMs to generate interaction data for training adaptive surveys is a novel contribution that showcases the transformative potential of recent AI developments in social science research. 2. **Empirical Validation**: The research is grounded in empirical testing, comparing synthetic and actual data distributions while also assessing the practical performance of the adaptive model, which enhances the validity of the findings. Weaknesses: 1. **Generality of Findings**: While the study focuses on a specific case (the Smartvote application in Switzerland), the findings may not be directly transferable to other contexts or demographics, limiting the generalizability of the conclusion. 2. **Dependence on LLM Limitations**: Although GPT-4 shows promise, the reliance on a single model may overlook variability in performance across different LLMs or the potential biases inherent in the training data of the model. Overall, the paper addresses a relevant and pressing issue in survey research and proposes a novel method to mitigate a significant challenge. However, potential limitations related to the specificity of the context and model dependency suggest a need for further research to confirm findings in diverse scenarios. Given the strengths and weaknesses, I would assign a score of **Score: 8**. This reflects a strong contribution to the field while acknowledging the need for broader application and further validation of the results.
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 1/10

### xVLM2Vec: Adapting LVLM-based embedding models to multilinguality using Self-Knowledge Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09313v1)
- **Authors**: Elio Musacchio, Lucia Siciliani, Pierpaolo Basile, Giovanni Semeraro
- **Abstract**: In the current literature, most embedding models are based on the encoder-only transformer architecture to extract a dense and meaningful representation of the given input, which can be a text, an image, and more. With the recent advances in language modeling thanks to the introduction of Large Language Models, the possibility of extracting embeddings from these large and extensively trained models has been explored. However, current studies focus on textual embeddings in English, which is also the main language on which these models have been trained. Furthermore, there are very few models that consider multimodal and multilingual input. In light of this, we propose an adaptation methodology for Large Vision-Language Models trained on English language data to improve their performance in extracting multilingual and multimodal embeddings. Finally, we design and introduce a benchmark to evaluate the effectiveness of multilingual and multimodal embedding models.
- **Summary**: **Summary**: The paper titled "xVLM2Vec: Adapting LVLM-based embedding models to multilinguality using Self-Knowledge Distillation" addresses the current limitations of language-vision models, which predominantly focus on English text and do not adequately accommodate multilingual and multimodal inputs. It proposes an innovative adaptation methodology leveraging Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) to enhance their capability in generating multilingual embeddings. The authors also introduce a benchmark that aims to evaluate the effectiveness of such multilingual and multimodal embedding models, thereby offering a systematic approach to assess performance across diverse languages and modalities. **Critical Evaluation**: The paper addresses a significant gap in the existing literature regarding the ability of embedding models to operate effectively across multiple languages and modalities. Given the increasing demand for models that can engage with non-English texts and various input forms (e.g., images or videos), the proposed adaptation methodology presents a valuable contribution.  **Strengths**: 1. **Novel Approach**: The integration of self-knowledge distillation for improving multilingual capabilities is a novel approach that can potentially enhance model performance and broaden applicability. 2. **Benchmark Creation**: The introduction of a benchmarking framework is a crucial step for evaluating multilingual models, which could inspire further research in this domain. 3. **Timeliness**: As the field moves towards more inclusive AI applications, the timing of this research is particularly pertinent. **Weaknesses**: 1. **Limited Empirical Validation**: The abstract does not provide insights into the empirical results or the robustness of the proposed methodology, which are essential to evaluate its effectiveness fully. 2. **Generality and Practicality**: The practical implementation of the proposed framework in real-world scenarios with diverse languages, particularly less-resourced languages, is yet to be examined. The viability and efficiency of using self-knowledge distillation at scale remain unclear. 3. **Scope of Language Diversity**: While the idea is to adapt the model to multiple languages, the paper's focus in terms of language selection needs to be articulated, as language diversity can significantly affect model performance. **Overall Assessment**: The paper demonstrates promise in advancing our understanding and capabilities of multilingual and multimodal embedding models, which are critical in today's globalized data landscape. However, to fully validate its claims, further empirical evidence and practical evaluation are needed. **Score: 7**  This score reflects the paper's noteworthy contributions in terms of novelty and potential impact while recognizing the need for further empirical substantiation and examination of practical applications.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Survey on Enhancing Causal Reasoning Ability of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09326v1)
- **Authors**: Xin Li, Zhuo Cai, Shoujin Wang, Kun Yu, Fang Chen
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have recently shown remarkable performance in language tasks and beyond. However, due to their limited inherent causal reasoning ability, LLMs still face challenges in handling tasks that require robust causal reasoning ability, such as health-care and economic analysis. As a result, a growing body of research has focused on enhancing the causal reasoning ability of LLMs. Despite the booming research, there lacks a survey to well review the challenges, progress and future directions in this area. To bridge this significant gap, we systematically review literature on how to strengthen LLMs' causal reasoning ability in this paper. We start from the introduction of background and motivations of this topic, followed by the summarisation of key challenges in this area. Thereafter, we propose a novel taxonomy to systematically categorise existing methods, together with detailed comparisons within and between classes of methods. Furthermore, we summarise existing benchmarks and evaluation metrics for assessing LLMs' causal reasoning ability. Finally, we outline future research directions for this emerging field, offering insights and inspiration to researchers and practitioners in the area.
- **Summary**: ### Summary: The paper titled "A Survey on Enhancing Causal Reasoning Ability of Large Language Models" provides a comprehensive review of the current state of research focused on improving the causal reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). While LLMs have demonstrated impressive performances in various language tasks, their intrinsic limitations in causal reasoning present significant challenges in fields requiring robust analytical skills, such as healthcare and economic analysis. This survey bridges a gap in the literature by detailing the challenges faced in enhancing causal reasoning in LLMs, proposing a novel taxonomy to categorize existing methods, comparing these methods, and summarizing benchmarks and evaluation metrics related to causal reasoning. Lastly, the authors suggest potential future research directions, aiming to inspire further investigation and innovation in this area. ### Critical Evaluation: **Strengths:** 1. **Addressing a Critical Gap**: The paper identifies a notable gap in the literature regarding the specific area of causal reasoning in LLMs, an important issue given the increasing reliance on these models in impactful decision-making domains.    2. **Systematic Review and Taxonomy**: The structured approach to reviewing the literature and proposing a novel taxonomy provides clarity and organization, which can help researchers navigate this rapidly evolving field.  3. **Comprehensiveness**: The paper encompasses a wide range of existing methods and evaluations, offering a solid foundation for understanding the current landscape of research. 4. **Future Directions**: By outlining future research paths, the paper encourages ongoing exploration and development, which could lead to innovative solutions and advancements in LLM capabilities. **Weaknesses:** 1. **Limited Novel Methodology**: While the review is extensive, the paper lacks a presentation of novel methodologies or experimental results. Its focus is predominantly on synthesizing existing knowledge rather than advancing it, potentially limiting its immediate impact. 2. **Potential Redundancy**: There is a risk of redundancy, as many existing papers may have addressed parts of this review individually, which could dilute the originality of synthesizing all information into a single document.  3. **Evaluation Metrics**: The discussion on evaluation metrics, while summarizing existing benchmarks, may not delve deeply enough into their limitations and real-world applicability. A more critical analysis could enhance the contributions of the paper. 4. **Contextual Impact**: The paper may not fully contextualize how improvements in LLMs' causal reasoning will translate into practical, beneficial outcomes in the aforementioned fields like healthcare and economics.  #### Conclusion: Overall, the paper represents a significant contribution to the understanding of challenges and opportunities in enhancing causal reasoning in LLMs. Its systematic approach, comprehensive literature review, and identification of future directions add value to the field; however, the lack of novel empirical contributions and potential redundancy somewhat diminish its impact.  **Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### CyberLLMInstruct: A New Dataset for Analysing Safety of Fine-Tuned LLMs Using Cyber Security Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09334v1)
- **Authors**: Adel ElZemity, Budi Arief, Shujun Li
- **Abstract**: The integration of large language models (LLMs) into cyber security applications presents significant opportunities, such as enhancing threat analysis and malware detection, but can also introduce critical risks and safety concerns, including personal data leakage and automated generation of new malware. To address these challenges, we developed CyberLLMInstruct, a dataset of 54,928 instruction-response pairs spanning cyber security tasks such as malware analysis, phishing simulations, and zero-day vulnerabilities. The dataset was constructed through a multi-stage process. This involved sourcing data from multiple resources, filtering and structuring it into instruction-response pairs, and aligning it with real-world scenarios to enhance its applicability. Seven open-source LLMs were chosen to test the usefulness of CyberLLMInstruct: Phi 3 Mini 3.8B, Mistral 7B, Qwen 2.5 7B, Llama 3 8B, Llama 3.1 8B, Gemma 2 9B, and Llama 2 70B. In our primary example, we rigorously assess the safety of fine-tuned models using the OWASP top 10 framework, finding that fine-tuning reduces safety resilience across all tested LLMs and every adversarial attack (e.g., the security score of Llama 3.1 8B against prompt injection drops from 0.95 to 0.15). In our second example, we show that these same fine-tuned models can also achieve up to 92.50 percent accuracy on the CyberMetric benchmark. These findings highlight a trade-off between performance and safety, showing the importance of adversarial testing and further research into fine-tuning methodologies that can mitigate safety risks while still improving performance across diverse datasets and domains. All scripts required to reproduce the dataset, along with examples and relevant resources for replicating our results, will be made available upon the paper's acceptance.
- **Summary**: **Summary**: The paper presents CyberLLMInstruct, a new dataset comprised of 54,928 instruction-response pairs designed for evaluating the safety of fine-tuned large language models (LLMs) in the context of cyber security. It addresses critical risks associated with using LLMs in this field, such as potential personal data leakage and the generation of malware. The dataset was created through an extensive multi-stage process, aiming to ensure real-world applicability. Testing the dataset involved seven different LLMs, wherein the authors applied the OWASP top 10 framework to evaluate safety. The results indicated that fine-tuning significantly compromised the safety of these models under various adversarial attacks. Despite this, the fine-tuned models demonstrated high accuracy (up to 92.50%) on the CyberMetric benchmark. The findings emphasize a trade-off between performance and safety, underscoring the necessity for more insightful research on fine-tuning methodologies. The study promises accessibility to the dataset and reproducibility of results upon acceptance. **Critical Evaluation**:  1. **Novelty**: The creation of CyberLLMInstruct is innovative, particularly as it specifically focuses on the intersection of large language models and cyber security—a field that is increasingly relevant given the proliferation of AI technologies. By addressing safety measures and the performance implications of fine-tuning, the authors highlight significant concerns that are often overlooked in existing literature. 2. **Significance**: The findings expose the critical vulnerabilities introduced when LLMs are fine-tuned for specific tasks in cyber security. The rigorous analysis using OWASP and performance metrics sets a foundation for further inquiry into how these models can be tuned without compromising safety. The dataset itself may serve as a valuable tool for both academic research and practical application, potentially guiding future developments in cyber security AI. 3. **Strengths**:    - The comprehensive nature of the dataset covering a wide range of cyber security tasks enhances its utility.    - The transparent approach to analysis and reproducibility strengthens the credibility of the findings.    - The trade-off between safety and performance acknowledged in the paper encourages a balanced view of LLM optimization. 4. **Weaknesses**:    - While the paper identifies significant safety concerns, it does not delve into potential solutions or improvements for better safety during the fine-tuning process.    - The focus on a limited set of models (seven LLMs) may not adequately represent the broader landscape of language models in cyber security.    - The implications of the findings on real-world applications, including specific recommendations for practitioners, could be more explicitly articulated. **Conclusion**: Overall, the paper makes a commendable contribution to the fields of AI and cyber security by revealing substantial safety concerns tied to the application of LLMs. While the nature of its findings may stimulate important discussions and future research, the paper could have benefited from a broader exploration of mitigative strategies. Nevertheless, its relevance to an urgent issue in the domain warrants a positive score. **Score: 8**.
- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Safer or Luckier? LLMs as Safety Evaluators Are Not Robust to Artifacts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09347v1)
- **Authors**: Hongyu Chen, Seraphina Goldfarb-Tarrant
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly employed as automated evaluators to assess the safety of generated content, yet their reliability in this role remains uncertain. This study evaluates a diverse set of 11 LLM judge models across critical safety domains, examining three key aspects: self-consistency in repeated judging tasks, alignment with human judgments, and susceptibility to input artifacts such as apologetic or verbose phrasing. Our findings reveal that biases in LLM judges can significantly distort the final verdict on which content source is safer, undermining the validity of comparative evaluations. Notably, apologetic language artifacts alone can skew evaluator preferences by up to 98\%. Contrary to expectations, larger models do not consistently exhibit greater robustness, while smaller models sometimes show higher resistance to specific artifacts. To mitigate LLM evaluator robustness issues, we investigate jury-based evaluations aggregating decisions from multiple models. Although this approach both improves robustness and enhances alignment to human judgements, artifact sensitivity persists even with the best jury configurations. These results highlight the urgent need for diversified, artifact-resistant methodologies to ensure reliable safety assessments.
- **Summary**: **Summary:** The paper "Safer or Luckier? LLMs as Safety Evaluators Are Not Robust to Artifacts" investigates the reliability of Large Language Models (LLMs) when utilized as automated evaluators for the safety of generated content. It presents a study of 11 LLM models, focusing on their self-consistency, alignment with human judgments, and vulnerability to input artifacts like apologetic or verbose language. The results indicate that biases in LLM judges can significantly misrepresent safety evaluations, with some artifacts causing a shift in evaluator preferences by up to 98%. Interestingly, larger models do not necessarily demonstrate superior robustness, and smaller models can sometimes resist specific artifacts better. The study also explores the potential of jury-based evaluations, which moderately improve the robustness and human alignment of safety judgments but still struggle with sensitivity to artifacts. The findings underscore the pressing need for more resilient and diversified approaches to ensure reliable safety assessments in content generation. **Critical Evaluation:** This paper provides a significant contribution to understanding the limitations of LLMs in safety evaluations, drawing attention to the pressing issue of artifact sensitivity that can critically influence the outcomes of safety assessments. The research's novelty lies in its empirical evaluation of different LLM judges, systematic investigation of biases, and the introduction of jury-based methodologies for addressing robustness concerns. **Strengths:** 1. **Empirical Investigation**: The study employs a comprehensive analysis of multiple LLMs, providing extensive empirical data on their performance in safety evaluations. 2. **Critical Insights**: The findings reveal critical vulnerabilities in LLMs, particularly around artifacts. This insight is vital for any future research aimed at improving LLM robustness in this context. 3. **Innovative Solutions**: The exploration of jury-based models offers a promising direction for improving evaluator outcomes, which could inspire further research and model design innovations. **Weaknesses:** 1. **Specificity of Artifacts**: While the paper covers a range of artifacts, it would benefit from diving deeper into the mechanisms by which specific types of artifacts affect judgment. More granular analysis could shed light on model behavior. 2. **Model Selection and Diversity**: The choice of LLMs could affect generalizability. If the selected models are too similar or too diverse, it could skew conclusions about size and robustness. 3. **Broader Impact and Application**: The implications of findings for real-world safety evaluations are not deeply explored, potentially limiting its applicability in practical scenarios. Considering the paper’s strengths in addressing a significant gap in the literature, providing empirical findings, and proposing a novel approach, I assign it a score of **8**. This reflects its impactful contributions to the field, while also acknowledging the areas where it could be strengthened and expanded upon in future works. **Score: 8**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### RetSTA: An LLM-Based Approach for Standardizing Clinical Fundus Image Reports
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09358v1)
- **Authors**: Jiushen Cai, Weihang Zhang, Hanruo Liu, Ningli Wang, Huiqi Li
- **Abstract**: Standardization of clinical reports is crucial for improving the quality of healthcare and facilitating data integration. The lack of unified standards, including format, terminology, and style, is a great challenge in clinical fundus diagnostic reports, which increases the difficulty for large language models (LLMs) to understand the data. To address this, we construct a bilingual standard terminology, containing fundus clinical terms and commonly used descriptions in clinical diagnosis. Then, we establish two models, RetSTA-7B-Zero and RetSTA-7B. RetSTA-7B-Zero, fine-tuned on an augmented dataset simulating clinical scenarios, demonstrates powerful standardization behaviors. However, it encounters a challenge of limitation to cover a wider range of diseases. To further enhance standardization performance, we build RetSTA-7B, which integrates a substantial amount of standardized data generated by RetSTA-7B-Zero along with corresponding English data, covering diverse complex clinical scenarios and achieving report-level standardization for the first time. Experimental results demonstrate that RetSTA-7B outperforms other compared LLMs in bilingual standardization task, which validates its superior performance and generalizability. The checkpoints are available at https://github.com/AB-Story/RetSTA-7B.
- **Summary**: ### Summary The paper titled "RetSTA: An LLM-Based Approach for Standardizing Clinical Fundus Image Reports" addresses the challenge of standardizing clinical fundus diagnostic reports to improve healthcare data integration. The authors develop a bilingual standard terminology to unify clinical terms and common diagnostic descriptions. They propose two models: RetSTA-7B-Zero, which is fine-tuned on a simulated dataset for clinical scenarios but has limitations regarding diversity in disease coverage, and RetSTA-7B, which enhances performance by incorporating a broader dataset, enabling it to perform report-level standardization effectively.
